  - **Validation** (`test/display-view-defocus.test.mjs`, `scripts/accept-display-view-defocus.mjs`, shared cases in `scripts/lib/display-view-defocus-cases.mjs`): against the disc's closed-form edge-spread function (axis-aligned edge on a cell boundary within 7.3e-15 linear at 1, 3 and 8 real pixels per display pixel; a 5° edge within the crossed-cell bound); against the in-focus view of the scene blurred beforehand by the capture's lattice convolution, 24 cases (focus 0.8× and 1.25×; f/2.8, f/8, f/16; 6 µm and 1.4 µm; Speed and Quality) at three scales, every one inside the #480 thresholds (72 renders): ΔE2000 at most 6.6e-11 on the exact area mean and 1.6e-7 on the stratified estimate, |Δ|/σ at most 1.3e-7. The capture with the option equals the capture of the pre-blurred scene at the same seed (every RAW code identical in 8 cases, Speed and Quality).
  - **Cost:** `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs --app` takes `DEFOCUS_RADII` (and opt-in f/2.8 rows 4 and 5); measured costs per radius are in `docs/DISPLAY_VIEW.md`, "Cost".
  - Docs: `docs/DISPLAY_VIEW.md` ("Defocus", limits, cost) and `docs/QUICK_START.md`.
- **`simulateCapture` renders `lens.defocus` out of focus (#492, step 2a).** No result changes without the option or in focus; every existing hash lock and fingerprint is unchanged.
  - **How:** an object-space convolution of the scene radiance on the site lattice with the geometric defocus disc, beside the diffraction convolution. The capture keeps its tabulated fast path, and regions, strips and DNG parts stay byte-identical to the whole frame. The `lens.defocus` assumption records `execution: "object-space-convolution"` and the geometric approximation.
  - **With `lens.diffraction`:** each spectral node's diffraction kernel is convolved with the disc, up to 32 + 64 = 96 sites. This is labelled as a further approximation, because the defocused wave PSF is not the Airy pattern convolved with the disc. Neither path uses the public 32-site limit of `calculateIrradianceConvolution`: the provider gathers only each band's own values (#419). The combined kernels cost at most 90 ms per spectral node (measured).
  - **Refused out of focus** with camera rotation, `lens.projection`, `lens.distortion` and `lens.vignetting`, which move the scene points off the lattice.
  - **Diagnostics:** `lens.defocus` joins `SIMULATE_CAPTURE_COST_OPTIONS` after `lens.diffraction`. `diagnostics.timingsMs.defocus` times the convolution; with diffraction the combined convolution is reported there, not under `diffraction`.
  - **Cap:** `DEFOCUS_DISC_KERNEL_MAXIMUM_RADIUS_SITES` stays at 64, now set from measured cost. On a 1024 × 683 tabulated capture (Node 24, one thread) a Siemens star took 0.37 s in focus, 2.9 s at R 32, 4.7 s at R 41 and 11.6 s at R 64. Dead leaves took 17.7 s at R 64, and Quality 52.9 s. The table is in `docs/QUICK_START.md`, "Optics".
  - **Validation** (`test/simulate-capture-defocus-render.test.ts`):
    - A vertical edge between lattice columns gives the exact circular-segment fraction (1e-12).
    - A slanted-edge capture's averaged profile follows the disc's closed-form edge-spread function, within 0.0093 of the step.
    - Against `lens.pupilSampling` at 4 × 8, 8 × 16 and 16 × 16 rays, the rays converge toward the continuous disc integral (RMS 0.021, 0.0049, 0.0035 for a 6-site disc). The lattice convolution differs from that integral by its point sampling of the edge: 0.112 max with point sampling, 0.035 with the 4 × 4 box.
    - The tabulated and environment paths give the same RAW.
    - Parts equal the whole frame, including `material-layers` scenes.
  - Docs: `docs/QUICK_START.md` (inputs table and Optics) and `docs/USAGE.md` (an example).

- **`lens.defocus: { model: "geometric-disc" }` and the geometric defocus disc on the site lattice (#492, step 1: the kernel and the option; no rendering yet).** No result changes without the option.
  - **The kernel** (internal, `src/optics/defocus-disc-kernel.ts`):
    - It takes the thin-lens blur circle c from `calculateDefocusCircle` and models it as a uniform disc of c/pitch sites.
    - Each site's weight is the exact area of the disc inside the site's cell, divided by the disc's area. The area is a closed-form circle–rectangle area, found by inclusion–exclusion over the cell's corners.
    - The weights are computed for one octant and mirrored, so the 8-fold symmetry holds bit for bit.
    - The weights are renormalized in float64, and the centre weight is set so that the exact sum is 1 to its last bit.
    - A disc inside one site gives the identity, and c = 0 gives no kernel.
    - The radius cap `DEFOCUS_DISC_KERNEL_MAXIMUM_RADIUS_SITES` = 64 is provisional, pending the cost measured in step 2. Past it the call fails and names the cap and the radius asked for.
  - **Portable arcsine:** `portableAsin` (internal, `src/core/portable-math.ts`) uses the Maclaurin series, with the half-angle identity above ½. It is within 2 ulps of `Math.asin` and bit-identical in every engine, so the weights are too.
  - **The option:** `simulateCapture`, `simulateDisplayView` and `simulateIdealReference` accept `lens.defocus`.
    - It resolves the disc at the resolved f-number and site pitch. The new `lens.defocus` assumption records the model, `scientificStatus: "approximation"`, the diameter in µm and in sites, and the kernel radius.
    - In focus, the image is byte-identical to the same request without the option.
    - Out of focus, the capture and the display view refuse the request with "does not yet support lens.defocus out of focus", naming the blur circle, rather than render it sharp. The ideal reference only records the disc.
    - It is refused together with `lens.pupilSampling` or `lens.psf`, because defocus would be counted twice. It can be combined with `lens.diffraction`.
    - With the option, the `lens.pupilSampling` default note points to it.
  - **Tests:** `test/defocus-disc-kernel.test.ts` and `test/simulate-capture-defocus.test.ts`.
    - A sampled straight edge (10 diameters from 0.3 to 128.9 sites, 4 angles, 4 offsets) is checked against the disc's closed-form edge-spread function, the circular-segment area fraction. The tolerance is the summed weight of the kernel cells the edge crosses, plus 1e-12.
    - Measured: 2.2e-13 for an edge on cell boundaries. Elsewhere the error was 28–33 % of that bound, 0.0040 at 128.9 sites.
    - Also tested: normalization, symmetry, the sub-site case, the cap, determinism (digest locks), the option's parse errors and refusals, and in-focus byte identity for the capture, display view and ideal reference.
    - `test/portable-math.test.ts` covers `portableAsin`.
  - Docs: `docs/QUICK_START.md` (inputs table and Optics).

- **Display view on the GPU for composite charts, the 2.3 elements and declared `material-layers` scenes (#491): the composite chart kernel's material lookup joined to the Speed kernel.** No result changes with `gpu: "off"`, and no CPU byte changes anywhere (hash locks unchanged). `prepareDisplayView(request, { gpu: "auto" })` now renders on the GPU, labelled `webgpu-f32`, a request whose scene is a composite chart, a 2.3 element, the sine Siemens star, a fixed-size chart (`chartSize`) or a declared `material-layers` scene (a chart under shape-mask layers: the form for an application's printed label); caller scene functions stay refused (`unsupported-on-backend`).
  - **Scenes:** `simulateCapture`, `simulateDisplayView`, `prepareDisplayView` and `createPlanarSceneRadianceProvider` accept `scene: { kind: "material-layers", sceneId, chart, layers }` directly; it is evaluated as its `callerScene` (`resolveChartMaterialLayers`), so every result, digest and byte equals passing that `callerScene` (tested). `DisplayViewGpuRequest.scene` gains `"material-layers"`; `DisplayViewGpuSetup` gains `layers` and `placement` (internal).
  - **The module** (`src/gpu/display-view-composite-material-module.ts`): the composite kernel's `material()` with its bound arithmetic and double-float32 as the Speed kernel's `material_at`, the scene's packed records at `@group(1)`, uploaded once per renderer and reused across a view's parts (four scenes kept, released by the new `DisplayViewGpuRenderer.dispose` when the prepared view is disposed). It recovers the exact query point from the kernel's float32 point (the site lattice: p / g picks the offset and integer site, then (x + c)·g in double-float32 from float64 constants), so the chirp decisions see the reference's point to about 2⁻⁴⁶; the host admits only lattices the recovery decides unambiguously (model checked at the corners within 2⁻⁴⁸, offsets told apart mod one site). The composite kernel's bounds and SAFETY factor apply unchanged and the Speed kernel's point contract (≤ 2E + 10⁻¹⁴) holds; no Speed bound changes (the argument and the measured 5.6·10⁻¹⁶ distance of the reference's points from the lattice: docs/WEBGPU.md, "The composite material module (#491)"). The 2.0 presets filling the frame keep the preset module.
  - **Device probe:** the composite kernel's probe (now `probeChartCompositeDevice`) gates the module once per renderer; on a device that fails it a render throws `unsupported-on-backend`, which the GPU session now turns into a CPU render with that label (or an error under `"require"`), retrying the probe on the next render; the preset module keeps serving the 2.0 presets there. A renderer's render-time `unsupported-on-backend` (coarse view, probe) falls back the same way.
  - **Measured** (float32 emulation and SwiftShader; 600 × 400 frame of 6 µm sites, 2 × 2 box, 203 × 135 view; one composite element of every kind under a label layer as `material-layers`, frame-filling and as a 0.042 m fixed-size chart at 0.63 m, plus 2.3 elements as plain charts): every component inside the a-priori bounds (at most 4.6 % of the expected-value bound and 29 % of the variance bound used), samples within one code, ΔE2000 at most 4.7e-5, at most 4.3e-4 noise σ, every #480 acceptance threshold met (100 % of pixels within ΔE2000 1); SwiftShader agrees with the emulation to every digit reported, flagged counts included. At the app's size (24 MP 6 µm, 2028 × 1352, the app's composite chart under a label layer as `material-layers`, emulation): ΔE2000 at most 4.2e-5, 87 of 8.2 million samples differ by one code, 1.3 % of display pixels flagged, every threshold met. Flagged display pixels (recomputed on the host, then exact): 4.2 % in the frame-filling composite (zone plate 59 %, label layer 51 %, grey ramp 16 %, bar star 9 %, others ≤ 4 %), 0.1 % in the fixed-size placement, 22.5 % for a 200-cycle zone plate alone, 0.5–2.1 % for dead leaves, the sine star and bar groups; the per-point flagged fraction is #477's (0.69 % over 197 544 points), a display pixel being flagged when any of its ~36–100 points is. Finding: the frame-filling lattice meets the label's integer and third-unit glyph coordinates exactly on some rows (3.8 % of the label's points flag); a cost, not an error. Details and the SwiftShader table in docs/WEBGPU.md.
  - **Timing:** unmeasured on a GPU (#475). Tests: `test/display-view-composite-gpu.test.ts` (emulation: the fixtures, flagged fraction per element, the #480 thresholds, the point recovery against the composite kernel at 197 544 points, records uploaded once, the probe gate, `prepareDisplayView` with `"auto"`/`"require"`, `"off"` byte-identical to the caller-scene form, `supports()`, lattice refusals, the WGSL), `test/display-view-gpu-option.test.ts` (render-time fallback, dispose), `test/webgpu/display-view.webgpu.ts` (SwiftShader), and an opt-in app-size case in `test/display-view-speed-gpu-full.test.ts` (`PHOTIVRA_DISPLAY_VIEW_APP_SCENE=1`). Docs: `docs/WEBGPU.md`, `docs/DISPLAY_VIEW.md`, `docs/QUICK_START.md`. Also removes a leftover merge-conflict marker from this changelog.
- **Display view: the Speed kernel on the GPU (#478), the renderer behind `prepareDisplayView(request, { gpu: "auto" | "require" })` for the chart presets.** No result changes with the default `gpu: "off"`, and none for requests the kernel does not cover (AHD, diffraction, caller scenes, coarser views: the CPU reference, labelled `unsupported-on-backend`); the GPU session registers the renderer when it loads unless a caller registered one. `src/gpu/wgsl-display-view-speed-kernel.ts` renders the display view's exact area mean without diffraction (the 2 × 2 box pixel aperture, bilinear demosaic, chart materials, the identity or positive Reinhard tone curve) one display pixel per invocation: the footprint's real pixels and overlap weights, every native site's expected electrons from the material lookup at the four query points and a float64 table, the readout moments and white clip, white balance, highlight clip, colour matrix, tone curve and clip to [0, 1] with the skewness term, the expected value, the Stein covariance with the closed-form B_c and the residual; the whole region in one dispatch when it fits the band. The host (`src/gpu/display-view-speed-gpu.ts`) computes every constant quotient in float64 (overlaps over the scale, axis sums over the squared scale, 1/Σw²) and finishes each pixel with the reference's own float64 functions: the quantization term, **the seeded noise draw (bit-identical to the CPU: the same stream, seed and portable normals)**, the Cholesky factor, the clipped centre, the sRGB encode and the rounding; a sample equals the CPU's except where the moments' bounded difference crosses a code boundary (at most one code). Clipped-normal moments are evaluated in standardized, cancellation-free forms (algebraically the reference's). The material lookup is a module (`DisplayViewGpuMaterialModule`, the seam of #477); `display-view-chart-material-module.ts` provides it for the eight 2.0 chart presets from the chart kernel's classification with a doubled safety factor, and flagged display pixels are recomputed with the reference. The renderer implements #481's `DisplayViewGpuRenderer` (`createDisplayViewSpeedGpuRenderer`, `displayViewSpeedGpuRendererFactory`, the self-check case `display-view:speed:checkerboard-13x9`) and refuses AHD, diffraction, caller scenes, coarse (stratified) views and footprints above 6 real pixels per axis with `unsupported-on-backend`. `DisplayViewSetup` gains an optional `gpu` field (the expected signal's input and the resolved chart) that the CPU renderer ignores.
  - **Bounds** (docs/WEBGPU.md, "Display view (Speed) on the GPU"): expected value 2·(γ_64 + 2^-40) relative plus a running absolute term for the clip steps (1 400E per clipped standard deviation per clip side), variance 2·(γ_80 + 2^-40) plus 36 000E per clipped variance, with E = 2^-23 (one WGSL rounding in either direction, #471); Reinhard adds 256E and 512E. Measured per pixel against the CPU reference on nine small fixtures and the 24 MP 6 µm frame at 2028 × 1352 (float32 emulation, and SwiftShader for the small ones): every component inside its bound using at most 4.1 % of it; ΔE2000 at most 4.3e-5, at most 3.3e-4 noise standard deviations, σ within 2.9e-4 relative at the white clip and 6e-6 elsewhere.
  - **Timing: TBD-TIMING.** No GPU time is measured (SwiftShader is not a GPU); the owner's hardware run (#475) records it. Measured on this host: the host finishing costs 1.22 µs per display pixel (3.4 s for a whole 2028 × 1352 view, one thread; the quantization term's `portablePow` 0.56 µs, the draws 0.28 µs, the encodes 0.22 µs), a floor of this design that the doc discusses.
  - Tests: `test/display-view-speed-gpu.test.ts` (emulation: bounds, ΔE2000 and σ units, flagged pixels, banding and regions, the draw, the support check, the self-check, `prepareDisplayView({ gpu: "auto" })` with the renderer registered), `test/display-view-speed-gpu-full.test.ts` (2028 × 1352), `test/webgpu/display-view.webgpu.ts` (SwiftShader, 10 tests). The display-view hash locks are unchanged.
- **Preview acceptance harness for the GPU display view, built against the CPU path (#480; the gate of #478 and #479 in the #473 epic).** Measurement tooling only; no result changes.
  - **The script:** `node scripts/accept-display-view-gpu.mjs` renders the app's display-view request (24 MP 6 µm, the 2 × 2 box, a caller material scene with the stand-in label, f/8; the five fixture charts at 2028 × 1352 and 2800 × 1867, Speed and Quality: 20 cases) with a "reference" producer and a "candidate" producer and compares the two noise-free expected images per pixel: ΔE2000 between the sRGB display colours (the engine's own CIEDE2000 and CIELAB with the sRGB space of `resolveRgbColorSpace("srgb")`) and the expected-value difference of each component in units of the reference's noise σ. It reports the fraction within ΔE2000 1, the maximum and mean ΔE2000, the maximum |Δ|/σ, the maximum difference in codes and the identical-pixel fraction, writes a difference image per case (the engine's PNG encoder, |Δ| in codes × 16, "Difference image, not a capture" in its text metadata) and prints pass or fail per threshold (parameters; defaults from #473: 99 % within ΔE2000 1, none above 2.3, no pixel beyond 1 σ). The "no structured difference at 1:1" check is the owner's visual review of the listed images. `--timing` adds the candidate's whole-view time and the reference timed as the benchmark does (prepare, sampled 96 × 96 parts, extrapolated whole view); the CPU pool's wall time is measured in the app, not here. `--record <name>` writes `docs/validation/<name>/` in the format of the October 26 record. The reference is always the CPU display view; `--candidate path/to/producer.mjs` selects another producer module (contract in `scripts/lib/display-view-cpu-producer.mjs`), where a GPU producer plugs in later.
  - **Self-check (the acceptance of #480):** with the CPU display view as the candidate, all 20 cases at both sizes show exact equality: every expected value, noise σ and drawn sample identical, ΔE2000 0 and |Δ|/σ 0 in every pixel, every threshold passed (`docs/validation/display-view-acceptance-self-check-2026-10-27/`; Node 24.21.0, one process per case, 4-vCPU Xeon under load from other work, 22 minutes; whole-view renders 17–23 s in Speed and 32–49 s in Quality; difference images not committed, all black, 52–100 KB each).
  - **Shared code:** the app request shape, chart, stand-in label and parts of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs --app` moved unchanged to `scripts/lib/display-view-app-request.mjs` (the benchmark re-exports them; its rows render the same request). The reduction, thresholds and difference image live in `scripts/lib/display-view-acceptance.mjs`, tested in `test/display-view-acceptance.test.mjs` on synthetic arrays and on colour identities computed from the definitions (no published CIEDE2000 test pairs are asserted, as `THIRD_PARTY.md` records). Docs: `docs/WEBGPU.md` and `docs/DISPLAY_VIEW.md`, "Preview acceptance".

- **Display view: the engine API through which `prepareDisplayView(...).render()` chooses the GPU or the CPU reference, and labels which ran (#481).** No result changes: the option defaults to `"off"`, and no GPU display-view renderer exists yet (#478, #479), so every render is still the CPU reference. This is the plumbing those kernels slot into.
  - **The option:** `prepareDisplayView(request, { gpu, gpuDevice })`. `gpu: "off" | "auto" | "require"` (default `"off"`) in the accelerator's words: `"auto"` is `backend: "webgpu-f32"` with `fallback: "cpu-reference"`, `"require"` is `fallback: "fail"`. `gpuDevice` is a worker's `navigator.gpu` to acquire a device from, or an `ImageKernelAccelerator` whose device the view shares and leaves to its owner; without it the engine acquires one device per prepared view, and the new `prepared.dispose()` releases it. The realm's `setWebGpuEnabled(false)` vetoes both GPU modes (`webgpu-disabled`). Until a renderer exists, `"auto"` runs the CPU reference labelled `unsupported-on-backend` and `"require"` fails with it, and no device is requested. The GPU code loads on demand, so bundles on the default do not contain it.
  - **Device handling:** one device per prepared view, shared by its parts; the request's `signal` aborts the acquisition and a render's `signal` its dispatch; GPU renders of one view run one at a time (concurrent renders wait); a lost device falls back to the CPU for that render and the rest of the view (`device-lost`), or fails with it in `"require"`.
  - **Conformance at run time:** before a device's first display-view render a short self-check runs once: the built-in cases run the existing `webgpu-f32` convolution kernel on two small seeded images against the float64 reference within its a-priori bound, then the renderer's own cases. A failure keeps the view on the CPU (`conformance-failed`) with the adapter and the failing case recorded.
  - **The label:** `diagnostics.fast` of a display view gains `execution` (`DisplayViewExecution`): `ImageKernelExecution`'s fields plus `gpu`, `adapter` (the device's `GPUAdapter.info`) and `conformance`. A CPU render keeps `backend: "cpu-reference"` and `assurance: "engine-reference"`; a GPU render reports `webgpu-f32`, its bound and adapter, `diagnostics.fast.assurance` becomes `approximation-with-a-priori-bound`, and the assumption `view.execution` is added, so an app can show "estimated on the GPU" and the comparison rule holds.
  - **The renderer contract** (internal, `src/facade/display-view-gpu.ts`, for #478): `DisplayViewGpuRenderer { id, supports(request), render(part), conformanceCases? }`, registered by a factory per device through `setDisplayViewGpuRendererFactory`. The only implementation is the test fake.
  - **Accelerator:** `ImageKernelAccelerator` gains `adapter` (its device's `GPUAdapter.info`, null for `cpu-reference`); new type `ImageKernelAdapterDescription`; `ImageKernelFailureReason` gains `conformance-failed`.
  - **Tests** (`test/display-view-gpu-option.test.ts`, with the fake WebGPU device and a fake renderer): the choice, the veto, the handle forms, the self-check and its failure paths, the fallback on loss, disposal, single flight, the abort and the label; `"off"` equals `simulateDisplayView` byte for byte and the display-view hash locks are unchanged. No hardware run yet (the development host has no GPU).
  - New root exports (types): `DisplayViewGpuMode`, `PrepareDisplayViewOptions`, `DisplayViewGpuFallbackReason`, `DisplayViewGpuConformance`, `DisplayViewExecution`, `DisplayViewFastReport`, `ImageKernelAdapterDescription`. Docs: `docs/DISPLAY_VIEW.md` ("Rendering on the GPU"), `docs/WEBGPU.md` ("The three GPU entry points"), `docs/USAGE.md`, `docs/QUICK_START.md`; API reference regenerated.

- **GPU chart radiance for every composite-chart element and for mask layers (#477; prerequisite of the GPU preview, #473).** No result changes: the float64 reference is untouched, and every GPU value is still the reference rounded once to float32.
  - **The kernel:** a second `webgpu-f32` chart-radiance kernel classifies `composite` charts, the 2.3 elements as plain charts (`dead-leaves`, `bar-groups` with numerals, `hyperbolic-wedge`, `zone-plate`, `slanted-squares`, `line-grid`, `fiducial`), the sine-profile Siemens star, and declared `material-layers` scenes; the eight 2.0 presets as plain charts keep their kernel. Every decision carries an a-priori bound (E = 2^-23 per WGSL rounding, 2.5 ULP per division, the point's low float32 part, 4× safety, 10^-14 slack) and flags the point for host recomputation when it is not decided. The zone plate's phase and the stars' sector tests run in double-float32 on hi + lo points; a star is a table of sector boundary directions with each sector's level read from the reference; cos is a Taylor polynomial in bound arithmetic. No WGSL `sin`, `cos` or `atan2` decides a material. A device probe checks round-to-nearest, unreassociated arithmetic once per accelerator and refuses the kernel otherwise (`unsupported-on-backend`). A scene's records are uploaded once per accelerator (keyed by the scene's data and aspect, four kept) and reused by later calls. `evaluateChartRadiance` consults `getWebGpuEnabled()` on every call (off: the reference runs, labelled `webgpu-disabled`).
  - **Declared material scenes:** `ChartMaterialLayerScene` (`kind: "material-layers"`: a chart under up to 16 shape-mask layers of polygons and annuli in the mask's own units, each with a 64 × 16 cell index) and `resolveChartMaterialLayers({ scene, aspect })`, which gives the float64 `materialAt`, the material list and `callerScene` (the same scene as a caller material scene for `simulateCapture`). `ChartRadianceBatchInput.scene` accepts it; the plan gains `layers` and `materials`. An application describes its printed label as a layer; the engine holds no label.
  - **Measured** (float32 emulation and SwiftShader, one element of every kind at an application-like placement, pixel centres of a 2028 × 1352 view): every unflagged value equalled the reference rounded once; flagged fractions 0 for twelve element kinds, 0.02 % for dead leaves and the hyperbolic wedge, 0.46 % for the zone plate and 0.50 % for the grey ramp, 0.013 % inside a label layer; on SwiftShader 0.21 % for the bar star (pixel centres on its diagonal sector boundaries) and 0.39 % for the zone plate, the rest at or below 0.31 % (the 1 % finding threshold is not reached). Details in `docs/WEBGPU.md`, "Composite charts, chart elements and mask layers". Finding (#471, not this kernel): at 8 of 198 recomputed bar-star points, Node's and Chromium's `Math.atan2` differ by one unit in the last place, so the two realms' float64 references disagree at points within that rounding of a sector boundary; the stage test records these as `realmDisagreements`. The per-fixture rollout extends to an all-elements composite (pinned method error measured on float64).
  - **Tests:** `test/chart-composite-gpu.test.ts` (per-element conformance and flagged fractions, the all-elements composite per element, plain 2.3 presets, layers resolution against the full shape test, malformed scenes, record packing and star tables, upload reuse and release, the probe refusal and retry, domain and limit refusals), `test/webgpu/stages.webgpu.ts` and `test/webgpu/rollout.webgpu.ts` (SwiftShader), `test/webgpu-rollout.test.ts` (CI subset). `test/chart-elements.test.ts` now expects the 2.3 presets to run on `webgpu-f32`.
  - New root exports: `resolveChartMaterialLayers`, `CHART_MASK_INDEX_GRAIN`, `CHART_MATERIAL_LAYER_LIMITS` and the types `ChartMaterialLayerScene`, `ChartMaskLayer`, `ChartMaskShape`, `ChartMaskPolygon`, `ChartMaskAnnulus`, `ResolvedChartMaterialLayers`, `ResolvedChartMaskLayer`, `ResolvedChartMaskShape`. Not done in this change: the hardware-device runs of #475 for the new kernel.
- **One boolean to turn the engine's GPU use on or off (#474).** No result changes: the GPU was already opt-in per call, and this adds the simple form of the request and one switch for the whole realm.
  - **The option:** `createImageKernelAccelerator({ webgpu: true })` is `backend: "webgpu-f32"` with `fallback: "cpu-reference"` (add `fallback: "fail"` to fail instead of falling back), and `{ webgpu: false }` is `backend: "cpu-reference"`. `webgpu` and `backend` exclude each other; `backend` is now optional when `webgpu` is given. The explicit form is unchanged.
  - **The switch:** `setWebGpuEnabled(false)` keeps every accelerator in the JavaScript realm on the CPU reference whatever callers ask. A request for `webgpu-f32` then runs the reference with the new `fallbackReason` `webgpu-disabled`, or throws `ImageKernelExecutionError` with that reason in fail mode, and no adapter is requested. `setWebGpuEnabled(true)` is the default; `getWebGpuEnabled()` reads it; anything but a boolean is refused with `InvalidConfigurationError`. A Web Worker has its own switch. An accelerator created while the switch was on keeps its device. Every GPU path added later (the preview and fast-capture work of #474) consults the same switch.
  - **Tests** (`test/image-kernel-accelerator.test.ts`): the boolean form resolves to the explicit form and rejects the mixed and non-boolean forms; with the switch off a working (fake) GPU is never asked for, the label and fail reason are `webgpu-disabled`, and the reference's bytes are returned; an accelerator created while on keeps its device after the switch is turned off.
  - New root exports: `getWebGpuEnabled` and `setWebGpuEnabled`; `ImageKernelAcceleratorOptions` gains `webgpu`; `ImageKernelFallbackReason` gains `webgpu-disabled`. Docs: `docs/WEBGPU.md` (Contract) and `docs/USAGE.md`.

- **WebGPU conformance suite in CI, a hardware record and per-stage timing summaries (#476, items 2–4).** Tooling and documentation only; no engine result changes.
  - **CI:** a new workflow, `.github/workflows/webgpu.yml` (job `webgpu conformance (SwiftShader)`), runs `npm run test:webgpu` with `PHOTIVRA_WEBGPU_REQUIRED=1` in Playwright's Chromium (pinned `playwright@1.63.0`, Chromium build 1243, installed in the job; no npm dependency added) on Google SwiftShader. It runs on pull requests that change `src/gpu/`, `test/webgpu/`, `vitest.webgpu*.config.ts` or the workflow, on every push to `main` and on manual dispatch, so its cost stays bounded (about four minutes a run). It checks the kernels' arithmetic against the float64 reference; it measures no GPU speed.
  - **Hardware record:** `docs/WEBGPU_DEVICES.md`, one row per hardware run (device, adapter, driver, browser, OS, engine commit, suite, result, timings file), seeded with the owner's Apple M5 Pro runs from #277 and #471 (6–24 MP timings of October 26 and 27, 2026, and the conformance run that passed 82 of 89 tests); the October 27 timing report is kept as `docs/validation/webgpu-devices/2026-10-27-m5-pro-bench.json`. `docs/WEBGPU.md` ("Running on hardware", "Timings at 6–24 MP", "Browser support and running the suite") and `docs/DEVELOPERS.md` link it.
  - **Timing summaries:** `npm run bench:webgpu` and `npm run bench:webgpu:hardware` print, after the JSON report, one line per frame and stage with the GPU milliseconds, the CPU milliseconds (with `PHOTIVRA_WEBGPU_TIMING_CPU=1`) and their ratio CPU ÷ GPU (`test/webgpu/timing-summary.ts`, computed from the report; the measurements are unchanged). Tested in `test/webgpu-timing-summary.test.ts` against the values posted on #277. Both WebGPU Vitest configurations now use the verbose reporter, because Vitest 5's default reporter hides a passing test's console output, which is where the suites print the adapter, the measured maxima and these summaries.

### Fixed

- **GPU: the composite chart kernel's device probe names what differs, and a device that fuses multiply-adds runs an fma product path (the owner's Apple M5 Pro run of October 27, 2026).** The first hardware run of the preview acceptance harness (`npm run accept:gpu:hardware`, Apple M5 Pro, Chrome, WebGPU on Metal, engine `main` at 14f9032) refused all 26 cases: every case is a `material-layers` scene, and the composite kernel's double-float32 probe failed ("does not evaluate the composite chart kernel's double-float32 arithmetic as written (reassociated or fused)"); 5 of the 26 were reported as the harness's cleanup error instead (below). The same day's `test:webgpu:hardware` run failed its 27 composite tests with the same refusal and passed the other 138. WGSL lets an implementation fuse a multiply and an add into one rounding and reassociate (W3C WGSL § 15.7.5 "Reassociation and Fusion"), and lets `fma` be unfused (§ 15.7.2, § 17.5.32); which of these Metal does is **not known until the owner's re-run** (a hypothesis, not a finding).
  - **Diagnostics.** The probe now also evaluates an fma TwoProduct and an fma double-word product, compares every component with its exact value (the double-word products with the written-order evaluation for each allowed contraction of their cross-term sum), and returns a `ChartCompositeProbeRecord` (new root types `ChartCompositeProbeRecord`, `ChartCompositeProbeComponent`, `ChartCompositeProbeMismatch`): the device class (`exact`, `contracted`, `reassociated`, `other`), the product path, whether `fma` is fused, and per component (TwoSum `s.l`, TwoProduct `m.l`, double-word add `a.l`, double-word multiply `q`, and the fma path's `mf.l`, `qf`) the count and input indices that differ, with the first eight differences' float32 bits. The refusal's message carries the same in one line. `DisplayViewExecution` gains `compositeProbe` (the record, or null when the render did not run the probe), on GPU renders and on the CPU fallback of `"auto"`; the error of `"require"` carries it as `probe`.
  - **The fma path.** TwoProduct as fma(a, b, −p) and a double-word product that never adds a rounded product (hi = fma(a.h, b.h, t), lo = fma(a.h, b.h, −hi) + t), so a fusion cannot change it; within 9u² of the exact product to first order (measured at most 5.3u²), inside the declared 2^-40. The composite kernel and the display view's composite material module are compiled with Dekker's path when its probe passes (so an unfused device keeps its results and flags bit for bit: no CPU byte changes, every hash lock holds), else with the fma path when it passes, else the device is refused as before. Every other place that might rely on unfused evaluation is shown unaffected or covered (docs/WEBGPU.md); the bound arithmetic stays valid under fusion, since a fused operation rounds once.
  - **Evidence** (emulation; no hardware yet): the fake device gains a fused-contraction mode (every multiplication an addition or subtraction consumes, through `let` bindings and calls, fused into one rounding, with `fma` fused or unfused) and a reassociation mode. Under contraction the old probe's criterion fails as on the M5 Pro, the new one reports `contracted` and picks the fma path, and composite radiance (44 800 queries per scene) and the Speed (8 fixtures; the six frame-filling ones on a 48 × 36 region) and Quality (2 regions) display views of the #491 fixtures render with no more flagged pixels than unfused: ΔE2000 at most 4.7 × 10⁻⁵ (Speed) and 1.9 × 10⁻⁴ (Quality), every component inside its bound, the #480 thresholds met. With an unfused `fma` the device is refused as `contracted`, under reassociation as `reassociated`. On SwiftShader the probe reports `exact` and keeps the Dekker path; SwiftShader's `fma` is not fused (`fmaFused: false`), so there the fma path is compiled (kernel and both display-view shaders) but its values cannot be checked. Tests: `test/chart-composite-contraction.test.ts`, `test/display-view-composite-contraction.test.ts`, `test/webgpu/stages.webgpu.ts` (the probe record and both paths on the device). The emulation's `bdiv` bound now uses the kernel's 5·E term (it had 2.5·E); no emulation test changed.
  - **The re-run found reassociation**, which the fma path does not survive: on the probe-fix commit the M5 Pro's probe reported class `reassociated` (TwoSum `s.l` 15 of 16 inputs, `m.l` 12, `a.l` 15, `q` 13, every differing low part zero; the fma components matched). See the next entry.
- **GPU: a rounding barrier keeps Metal's compiler from rewriting the composite chart arithmetic; composite charts and `material-layers` scenes now render on the GPU on the Apple M5 Pro.** Every operation of the double-float32 error-free transforms and every value of the bound arithmetic is wrapped in `ob(x) = bitcast<f32>(bitcast<u32>(x) ^ zero)`, `zero` a uniform word the host sets to 0 (`params.zero`, `lattice.zero`, the probe's `probe_zero`), which a compiler cannot see through, so it can neither regroup nor fuse across it (W3C WGSL § 15.7.5). XOR with 0 changes no bit: on a device that already evaluates as written (SwiftShader, the emulation) values, flags and bytes are unchanged, and no hash lock changes. **Measured on the M5 Pro** (one run each, engine `9ce630b` with the barrier): the probe reports `exact` on the Dekker path, every component bit-exact; both composite scenes equal the reference at every unflagged query (77 640 each; 75 and 81 flagged); `npm run accept:gpu:hardware` PASS, 26 of 26 on `webgpu-f32` (100.00 % within ΔE2000 1, largest ΔE2000 0.0010, largest |Δ|/σ 0.002, 8-bit codes equal to the reference's in every pixel); `npm run test:webgpu:hardware` 167 of 167. Render times of that run (informational, one run): GPU 2.2–16.3 s per view against the CPU reference's 9.1–27.3 s, except dead leaves in Quality (21.7 s against 23.6 s at 2028 wide, 28.4 s against 25.1 s at 2800 wide). Tests: `test/chart-composite-barrier.test.ts` (every shader variant reads its zero word, no operation of the transforms is unwrapped, the host writes 0). Another device is still judged by its own probe.
- **Tests: the composite emulation fits the coverage shards again.** The arithmetic models of `test/helpers/fake-webgpu-composite.ts` had made the default (round-to-nearest) emulation about 5.5 times slower under V8 coverage (`test/display-view-composite-gpu.test.ts` 95 s → 519 s), and the new contraction tests rendered full views (1 370 s), so two coverage shards hit their 15-minute limit. The nearest model now runs its bound arithmetic and double-word blocks without a call per operation (519 s → 111 s; the same arithmetic, operation for operation), and `test/display-view-composite-contraction.test.ts` drops the unfused renders that `test/display-view-composite-gpu.test.ts` already makes and renders the six frame-filling Speed cases on a fixed 48 × 36 region, where the contracted render must flag no more pixels than the unfused one (measured: equal), keeping the full view for the fixed-size charts (1 426 s → 192 s for both contraction files).
- **`npm run test:webgpu` and the acceptance harness: the browser cleanup no longer fails a case (`ENOTEMPTY` on macOS, the same M5 Pro runs).** `test/webgpu/chromium.ts` removed the Chromium profile right after SIGKILL, while the browser's processes could still write to it. The cleanup (`cleanupBrowser`) now waits for the process to exit (up to 10 s), removes the profile with five retries 200 ms apart, and logs a failure instead of throwing, so a cleanup problem can no longer turn a case's result into an error. Tests: `test/webgpu-chromium-cleanup.test.ts` (a fake child that exits late, a removal that fails, a process that never exits).
- **The acceptance harness prints the composite probe per case**: its class, the product path the composite kernel ran with, and the differing components with their inputs, on a GPU render and on a refusal alike, and records it with each case's execution label.
- **GPU display view (Speed): the variance bound names the output quantization term (#496).** The host evaluates the quantization variance q(m) = s(e)²·Δ²/12 at the GPU's mean, so it moves by up to 6.04·Δ²/12 per unit of mean error; the Speed host now adds Σ(w/A)²·(6.04·Δm + the segment-join jump)·Δ²/12, doubled, to each display pixel's absolute variance term, as the Quality host does (#479). Before, this was covered only incidentally by the γ_80 relative term. No computed value, CPU result or hash lock changes; only `varianceBound.absolute` and the per-pixel variance bounds grow. On the Speed fixtures every component stays inside its bound; the largest fraction of the variance bound used falls from 4.0 % to 3.9 % on the chart presets and from 4.1 % to 4.0 % on the composite fixtures (Reinhard at the white clip: 28.7 % before and after). Docs: `docs/WEBGPU.md`, the Speed derivation table. Test: `test/display-view-speed-gpu-quantization.test.ts`.
- `npm run test:webgpu`: the Chromium driver now polls the DevTools target list for the first page instead of reading it once; Chromium can print its DevTools endpoint before `about:blank` is registered, which failed one CI run of the suite with "Chromium exposed no page target" (#488).
- **WebGPU sensor stage and Siemens star: the `webgpu-f32` kernels no longer depend on correctly rounded division or on the order of float32 sums, and their margins are derived from the WGSL accuracy table (#471).** The first hardware run (Apple M5 Pro, Metal) found unflagged sites that differed from the reference in every regime with a non-unit gain, and one Siemens-star boundary point in the wrong sector. The CPU reference, its hashes and every `cpu-reference` result are unchanged.
  - **Assumptions, restated.** `docs/WEBGPU.md` now states the W3C WGSL "Floating Point Accuracy" table (§15.7.4, with citation) as the only assumption: WGSL fixes no rounding direction, so one `+ − ×` is within one ULP (ε = 2^-23 relative, not 2^-24); `/` is 2.5 ULP; `sqrt` inherits `1/inverseSqrt` (4.5 ULP); `exp`, `log` have their table bounds; and an implementation may reassociate. The doc derives every sensor-stage margin from it in a new section, "Sensor-stage margins (derivation)".
  - **Sensor kernel.** The integer part of signal/gain and of the digital-gain product is now formed in 32-bit integer arithmetic from host-computed 12-bit pieces (mantissa, exponent, exact scale), so no exact floating-point split depends on the evaluation order; the INL offset uses host-computed segment slopes instead of a division; the Box–Muller and PTRS quotients carry the 2.5 ULP bound; every multi-term sum carries the order-independent bound (n − 1)·ε·Σ|terms|; and the margins are written in ε. The flagged share rises modestly: on the SwiftShader conformance corpora (256 × 128 sites) from 95–117 to 109–144 flagged sites per regime (0.3–0.4 % → 0.3–0.4 %), and the inexact digital gain from 804 to 823 (its flags are the exact half-code ties of 1.3 × integer); the fixed-pattern variants from 104–129 to 131–154 and 1443 to 1466. No unflagged site differs.
  - **Siemens star.** The sector is found by a binary search on cross-product signs against the host's float64 boundary directions, in the half-plane chosen by the sign of v; the kernel calls no transcendental function. The failing point itself turned out to be a realm difference, not a GPU error: ECMAScript does not fix the last bit of `Math.atan2`, Node 24 and Chrome 153/155 differ by one ulp there, and the suite's point placed exactly on a sector boundary classifies light in Node and dark in the browser (SwiftShader and Metal alike). The GPU had flagged it and recomputed it with the browser's reference. The conformance suite now compares against the reference of the browser realm and reports realm differences, which must be flagged boundary points; the doc records the dependence.
  - **Evidence.** `test/webgpu-adversarial-arithmetic.test.ts`: the fake device's float32 emulation gains an adversarial arithmetic (random rounding direction for every `+ − ×` and conversion, division off by up to 2.5 ULP, `sqrt`, `log`, `exp` at their table bounds, random summation order and a reassociated INL step); on the six sensor regimes, their fixed-pattern variants (128 × 64 sites, three seeds) and all eight chart presets no unflagged site or point changes, and a dense Siemens-star boundary sweep (every boundary at twelve radii, exact, one float32 ulp off, 10^-9 and 10^-5 off, the u-axis, the ±π cut and signed zeros) equals the reference. The SwiftShader suite (`npm run test:webgpu`) passes (89 tests). **The hardware re-run on the M5 Pro is pending** (#475); the sensor failures do not reproduce on SwiftShader, which divides exactly, so for them the emulation is the evidence until then.
  - Internal layout change only: the sensor kernel's auxiliary table (`SENSOR_AUX`, three words per piece, INL slopes) and one more uniform (the largest INL knot code). A digital gain outside [2^-60, 2^60] is now `unsupported-float32-domain`.

## 2.10.0 — published October 27, 2026

A minor release: one exact-or-fast flag for every slow option (no option has a fast method yet, so every result is still exact), export and import of the display view's AHD correction states so workers can share them, and faster display-view prepares and parts and faster Quality captures. Samples, RAW codes and files are the same as 2.9.0; captures and display views gain the `diagnostics.fast` report.
- **New:** `fast`, `SIMULATE_CAPTURE_FAST_OPTIONS` and `diagnostics.fast` (#395, stage 2). `serializeDisplayViewCorrectionStates`, `bindDisplayViewCorrectionStates` and three `PreparedDisplayView` methods share the AHD correction states between workers (#447); no speed-up is claimed until the app measures it.
- **Faster prepare** (Node, one thread): `prepareDisplayView` after an aperture change takes 54–67 ms instead of 378–430 ms in Quality (#443), and after an ISO, white-balance or shutter change 9–16 ms instead of 37–45 ms at 6 µm f/11 (#446).
- **Faster parts and captures** (Node, one thread, each against the build before it): Quality display-view part medians −8 to −14 % (#441), −3 to −11 % (#445) and −3 to −8 % (#442); Speed parts −4 to −7 % (#442) and up to −9 % whole-view CPU (#444); the AHD demosaic stage −40 % (#448); the app's Quality 96 × 64 detail tile median 128 → 115 ms (#449, first part) and 116 → 95 ms (#449, second part), then 102 → 90 ms with the verification sites' chain re-validating less (#453). Browser wall time is not measured.
- **Compatibility:** eight new root exports (1,742); new optional `fast` fields and an `output.demosaic` record form; `lens.diffraction`'s `aperture` key is deprecated in favour of `kind`. Query points use a portable `hypot3`, unchanged on V8 (#452).

### Added

- **One exact-or-fast flag for every slow option (#395, stage 2): `fast`, `SIMULATE_CAPTURE_FAST_OPTIONS` and `diagnostics.fast`.** No option has a fast method yet, so every result is the same as before.
  - **The flag:** `fast?: boolean` on the `simulateCapture` request (and so on `simulateDisplayView` and `prepareDisplayView`, which take the same request). `false` or omitted is exact, the engine reference; `true` asks each listed option for its fast method, a measured, bounded approximation. An option's record `fast` overrides the top-level flag for that option only.
  - **The options:** `SIMULATE_CAPTURE_FAST_OPTIONS` lists `lens.diffraction`, `sensor.antiAliasing`, `sensor.pixelAperture.box` and `output.demosaic.ahd`. Each takes a record with `fast`: `lens.diffraction` `{ kind: "circular-aperture", kernelRadiusSamples?, fast? }`, `output.demosaic` (new record form) `{ kind: "bilinear" | "ahd", fast? }`, and the existing `sensor.antiAliasing` and `sensor.pixelAperture` records gain `fast?`. An option without a fast method accepts the flag, runs exact and reports `false`.
  - **The report:** captures and display views carry `diagnostics.fast = { requested, applied, assurance }`: whether `fast: true` was given anywhere, each listed option that ran (true where its fast method ran) and `engine-reference` or `approximation-with-a-priori-bound`. The WebGPU image-kernel execution record gains `fast` (`false` for `cpu-reference`, `true` for `webgpu-f32`).
  - **Same output:** every request gives the same samples, RAW and files as before; a new test (`test/fast-flag-convention.test.ts`) checks each listed option with the flag on, off and overridden, and enforces the convention documented in `docs/API_STYLE.md`, "Exact and fast modes".
  - New root exports: `SIMULATE_CAPTURE_FAST_OPTIONS` and the types `SimulateCaptureFastOption`, `SimulateCaptureFastReport` and `SimulateCaptureDemosaicRecord`.

- **Display-view benchmark and validation on a device-pixel request (#454).** Measurement and documentation only; no result changes.
  - **Benchmark:** `node scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs --app` times a 2028 × 1352 view of a 24 MP, 6 µm frame with the 2 × 2 box, a caller material scene and a stand-in label, prepared once and rendered in 96 × 96 parts centre first, in Speed and Quality at f/8 and f/11. It prints per-part medians and maxima, µs per display pixel, the estimation method, AHD-corrected pixels, the load average, the Node version and the commit; with `DIST_B` it runs a second build interleaved. Measured on 2.9.0 (Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon): Speed about 0.09 s per part, Quality about 0.18 s, prepare 0.2 s and 0.5 s. The 2.6.0 whole-view table is now labelled as the 2.6.0 record.
  - **Validation set F:** `node scripts/validate-display-view.mjs --set F` (outside the default run) checks the view at device-pixel widths on the full-frame (2028 wide) and medium-format (4000 wide) sensors. Speed passes every limit. Quality misses two, now recorded in `docs/DISPLAY_VIEW.md` and `docs/validation/display-view-2026-10-26/`: the predicted noise is 1.13–1.20 of the reference's (the known limit, #379), and at the Siemens star's centre the view's AHD result differs from the capture's by 5.1 codes RMS, at most 34 (full frame).
  - **Test:** parts of a prepared device-pixel view of a caller material scene with the box and the Quality options equal separate `simulateDisplayView` calls, by SHA-256 (`test/display-view-prepared.test.ts`).

- **Display view: export, import, collect and compute of the AHD low-signal correction states, so workers can split and share them (#447).** No result changes.
  - **Why:** each correction state costs 27–30 ms (2048 drawn neighbourhoods through the AHD steps; Node, one thread, measured for #447), and the states are kept per JavaScript realm. Each Web Worker of an app computed the same states again, and each worker's first part waited for all of them: 897 states for one 48 × 48 part at the centre of the app's Quality view at 6 µm ISO 400.
  - **The API:** `serializeDisplayViewCorrectionStates()` returns one `{ key, gridKeys, values }` entry per cached setup (an `Int32Array` of grid keys and a `Float64Array` of three values per state, 28 bytes per state, both transferable). `bindDisplayViewCorrectionStates(entries)` adds the states a setup does not hold yet and returns the count added. It checks every entry first (a non-empty key, grid keys in [0, 97³), 3 × that many finite values) and fails with an `InvalidConfigurationError`, leaving the cache unchanged. The cache's bounds apply as in a render (4 setups, 65 536 states each), so import only keys a prepared view reported. `PreparedDisplayView` gains `correctionKey()` (null without AHD), `collectCorrectionStates(view)` (the grid keys of every state a render of the view reads, from a render pass that records them instead of computing them; it returns no image and leaves the cache untouched) and `computeCorrectionStates(gridKeys, { signal })` (computes the given states as a render does, stores them and returns them; it yields and can be aborted like `render`). Documented in `docs/DISPLAY_VIEW.md`, "AHD at low signal", with a two-worker example in `docs/USAGE.md`.
  - **Same output:** renders are unchanged. The state computation moved, unchanged, from the render into a shared function used by the render and `computeCorrectionStates`. Samples, expected values, noise, every diagnostic (including `sceneLookups` and `ahdCorrectedPixelCount`) and the assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build on 12 renders: the issue's comparison set (the app's Quality request at ISO 3200 at a frame corner and in the chart area, at 1.4 µm in 16 bits at the centre, at 6 µm ISO 400 on the coarse 192 × 128 view at the centre and the bottom-right corner, and at 6 µm ISO 200 and ISO 400 at the centre in 8 and 16 bits; spectral patches with mirror borders at 2 µm ISO 1600; a radiance-function scene at 1.4 µm ISO 3200; dead leaves with the Reinhard tone curve at 4 µm ISO 6400 in 16 bits). Each of the 12 also has the same hash when its states are collected, computed in two halves in two cleared caches (one half in reversed order), exchanged by export, `structuredClone` and import in reversed order, and then rendered; the collect pass returned exactly the states of the cold render (0 to 914 per render). The existing hash locks pass unchanged. New tests in `test/display-view.test.ts` render a region after importing a neighbour's states in reversed order and after merging two realms' sets, and compare it with a cold render. They also check that the collect pass returns exactly the states a cold render computes, that two realms hold the same bits for common states, and that the import rejects malformed states and leaves the cache unchanged.
  - **Measured** (the app's Quality request, one 48 × 48 part at the view centre, a fresh process per run, against the previous build a58cbe8, three rounds alternating the builds; Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon, load average 0.6–1.0): the per-state cost (cold minus warm render time over the states) is unchanged, 25.10–25.30 → 25.07–25.29 ms at 6 µm ISO 400 (764 states) and 27.4–30.9 → 27.4–29.2 ms at ISO 200 (32 states). The collect pass took 209–217, 126–132 and 76–77 ms in three successive calls, against 129–139, 100–105 and 94–98 ms for a warm render of the same part. `structuredClone` of the exported set (764 states, 21,392 bytes) took 10–11 µs at the median and 15–16 µs at the 90th percentile of 200 clones; serializing it took 0.19–0.21 ms. No speed-up is claimed: any gain in the app depends on its workers splitting and exchanging the states (Photivra/apps#113), which has to be measured in the browser.
  - **Exports:** `serializeDisplayViewCorrectionStates` (the export), `bindDisplayViewCorrectionStates` (the import) and the types `DisplayViewCorrectionStateSet` and `DisplayViewCorrectionStateKeys`; root exports grow from 1,738 to 1,742. The two functions take the `serialize` and `bind` verb families of `docs/API_STYLE.md` (no new verb family).

### Faster, same output

- **Display view, exact path with diffraction (device-pixel views such as the app's Quality parts): each native site's value, material and readout moments are computed once per render (#441).**
  - **What changed:** a real pixel's demosaic reads 27 native sites (AHD) or 7 (bilinear), and each site was read through the expected signal and clipped to its readout moments again for every real pixel that reads it, about 25 times per site on the app's Quality request. A per-render ring of native rows over the part's columns now keeps each site's expected electrons, chart material and moments after its first read; a new internal `markReads` tells the expected signal's window which of its sites a real pixel read from the ring, so `diagnostics.sceneLookups` is the count reading them would give. Also: no jitter stream on the exact path (it never draws one), the site moments read the slot arrays instead of taking boxed arguments, a record's unwritten fields are zeroed one by one instead of filling the whole record, and two-argument maxima replace a four-argument `Math.max`. The ring is used only where the expected signal fills its windows (chart materials with diffraction); coarse (stratified) views, views without diffraction (Speed, where the ring measured 2–4 % slower on the `--app` request) and radiance scenes with diffraction keep the per-read path and allocate no ring. Memory: 7 arrays of about 44 bytes per ring entry; for a 96 × 96 part at 3 native pixels per display pixel about 16 native rows × 300 columns, about 0.2 MB per render, and never more than 2^18 entries (about 11.5 MB; above that the ring is off).
  - **Same bytes:** samples, expected values, noise, every diagnostic (including `sceneLookups` and `siteEvaluations`) and the assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build on 224 renders: the `--app` request of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs` in Speed and Quality at f/8 and f/11 (centre, corners, edges and the label area), 16-bit output, coarse stratified views (K = 4), mirror borders, ISO 3200 and a 1.4 µm sensor with the AHD correction, dead leaves with the Reinhard tone curve and a radiance-function scene, rendered in sequence, separately and concurrently. The existing hash locks pass unchanged. A new test (`test/display-view-site-ring.test.ts`) checks overlapping and adjacent parts of one prepared view in both orders and concurrently against separate calls and the whole view, with mirror borders and the AHD correction, and that `markReads` gives a filled and a direct window the lookup count and later reads of `site`.
  - **Measured** (Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon, load average 1.0–1.7, `DIST_B=… node scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs --app`, 30 of 294 parts, 3 repeats per build, two separate runs, builds alternated): Quality part medians 169–194 → 148–164 ms at f/8 and 177–187 → 154–164 ms at f/11 (−8 to −14 %); Speed, where the ring is not used, within ±3 %. Browser wall time is not measured here.
- **Display view prepare and captures: diffraction kernels are kept by value, so an ISO, white-balance or shutter change reuses them (#446).**
  - **What changed:** the per-node site diffraction kernels (`prepareDisplayView`, the capture's tabulated RAW path and the object-space diffraction convolution) were cached by the identity of the PSF record, which the facade builds anew for every request, so every prepare resolved them again. They are now kept by value: the key is the whole `createDiffractionLimitedPsfProfile` input (aperture, focal length, f-number, focus, spectral nodes, pitch, kernel radius, field extent, profile id), the PSF record's other fields and the query's wavelength, pitch and radius. The last four optics settings are kept (least recently used out first; about 2.7 MB with 20 spectral nodes at the 32-site radius); each caller still gets its own copy. An aperture, focal-length, focus, pitch or radius change resolves the kernels again.
  - **Same bytes:** a kept kernel is the same pure function of the same inputs; a new test (`test/site-diffraction-kernel-cache.test.ts`) checks that kept and freshly resolved kernels are equal element by element (`Object.is`) for pitches 1.0–8.4 µm and f/1.4–f/22, that a change of any input misses, that writing to a returned kernel changes nothing later, and that prepares after an ISO, white-balance or shutter change and captures after a prepare render the bytes of a cold run. Samples, RAW codes, diagnostics without timings and assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build on 55 cases (229 digests, including the whole result without timings): the `--app` request of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs` in Quality through ISO, white-balance, shutter, f-number and focus changes, the 1.4 µm sensor at f/8, f/22 at 6 µm, captures on the tabulated path and on the object-space diffraction path.
  - **Measured** (Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon, load average about 1.2, the app-request shape in Quality, five rounds in one process alternating the two builds; ranges): `prepareDisplayView` after an ISO change 37–45 → 9–16 ms at 6 µm f/11, 76–84 → 10–12 ms at 6 µm f/22 and 241–276 → 15–19 ms at 1.4 µm f/11; after a white-balance change 38–41 → 9–11 ms, 77–88 → 10–12 ms and 241–279 → 16–17 ms; after a shutter change 37–41 → 9–13 ms, 76–121 → 10–12 ms and 234–305 → 15–19 ms. Browser wall time is not measured here.

- **Display view and tabulated capture: the scene lookup count of a filled window is computed once per group shape (#445).**
  - **What changed:** `diagnostics.sceneLookups` (and the capture's tabulated `providerEvaluationCount`) counts, for each filled window (chart materials with diffraction), the lattice points the per-pixel lazy caches would have looked up, a box dilation of the window's computed sites per lattice group. The count is a function of those sites and each group's rectangle and cache sizes only, so a group shaped like an earlier one now takes that group's count (on the app's Quality request all four groups have one shape), in both the bit-mask and the general branch; the OR of the site rows that reach a cache row comes from prefix and suffix ORs of the row masks, computed once per window; and a window whose every site was computed, with no site read outside it (the capture path's windows), counts every point of its caches without dilating. No image value is computed by this count.
  - **Same bytes and the same count:** samples, every diagnostic (`sceneLookups` compared explicitly) and the assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build on the `--app` request of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs` in Speed and Quality at f/8 and f/11 (centre, corners, edges and the label area), coarse stratified views (K = 4), mirror borders, ISO 3200, a 1.4 µm sensor, dead leaves and a radiance-function scene. A new test (`test/expected-signal-lookup-count.test.ts`) compares the open and closed windows' counts with a copy of the previous algorithm and with a brute-force count of the definition over seeded random window sizes on both sides of the 31-site bit-mask limit, windows at the raster edges, kernel radii, group shapes (four-spot, two-spot, different and random offsets), partial, banded and fully read windows, reads outside the window, repeated reads and `markReads`. The existing hash locks pass unchanged.
  - **Measured** (`DIST_B=… node scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs --app`): Quality part medians 149–152 → 132–144 ms at f/8 and 155–158 → 138–156 ms at f/11 (−3 to −11 %; Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon, load average 1.3, 30 of 294 parts, 3 repeats per build, builds alternated, against #441); Speed has no filled windows and is unchanged (84–91 → 85–90 ms). Browser wall time is not measured here.
- **Display view render loop: typed and flat per-render tables, so V8 keeps its optimized code (#442).**
  - **What changed:** in `renderDisplayView` the readout tables (`k`, `dark`, `c0`, `readStd`) and the homogeneity white (`labWhite`) are built with `Float64Array.from` instead of `.map`; the per-phase candidate tables are read from flat arrays of typed arrays (`phase * 2 + candidate`, and `* 3 + channel` for the slots and weights) instead of nested arrays; and a site's window offset is read from two per-render `Int32Array`s (`(slot % side) - reach`, `((slot / side) | 0) - reach`, computed once) instead of a modulo and a division per site read. The number of candidates (1 bilinear, 2 AHD) is now checked to be the same for every phase. Keyed loads on the earlier tables met several array shapes, and V8 discarded the whole function's optimized code on them (`wrong map` deoptimizations) several times per view: with `node --trace-deopt --trace-opt`, one run each against #441, 15 → 2 over 36 parts of the app's recorded Quality request (completed TurboFan compiles of the function 14 → 6), 9 → 2 over 24 Quality Siemens-star parts and 16 → 4 over 24 Speed ones. The `exit from OSR'd inner loop` deoptimizations vary from run to run and are not claimed. Memory: two `Int32Array`s of at most 49 entries and four small arrays of references per render.
  - **Same bytes:** the tables hold the same doubles and integers and every operation runs on the same values in the same order. Samples, expected values, noise, every diagnostic (including `sceneLookups` and `siteEvaluations`) and the assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build on the 224-render set of #441 (the `--app` request of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs` in Speed and Quality at f/8 and f/11 at the centre, corners, edges and label area, prepared parts in sequence and concurrently; 16-bit; coarse stratified views, K = 4; mirror borders; ISO 3200 and a 1.4 µm sensor with the AHD correction; the Reinhard tone curve; a radiance-function scene). The existing hash locks pass unchanged. A new test (`test/display-view-render-tables.test.ts`) checks the offset tables against the modulo and division and that the flat tables hold the nested tables' own arrays.
  - **Measured** (Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon, load average 1.3, 30 of 294 parts, 3 repeats per build, builds alternated, `DIST_B=… node scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs --app`, against #441): Quality part medians 148–156 → 143 ms at f/8 and 154–155 → 149–151 ms at f/11 (−3 to −8 %); Speed 84–87 → 79–81 ms at f/8 and 83–91 → 80–84 ms at f/11 (−4 to −7 %). Browser wall time is not measured here.

- **Display view without diffraction (the Speed path): one-material samples are replayed there too (#444).**
  - **What changed:** the display view replays the recorded terms of a sample whose demosaic sites all read one chart material (the one-material memo), but the expected signal reported the material only on the diffraction path, so every Speed sample was computed in full. Without diffraction a site now records whether all its query points (four with the 2 × 2 box) read one material, and the signal's direct ring and window cache keep that material beside the site's value, so the memo applies to chart materials (presets and caller material scenes) on the Speed path. Radiance scenes are not tracked. A sample whose sites read two materials cannot be replayed, so from the first site of a second material on it takes the site statistics at once instead of in a second pass, which limits the extra work on parts with no one-material samples (the Siemens-star centre).
  - **Same bytes:** a one-material site sums the same operands in the same order as every other such site of its channel, so a replayed sample adds exactly the terms it would have computed; a site's statistics depend on that site only, so taking them earlier changes no bit. Samples, every diagnostic (including `sceneLookups` and `siteEvaluations`) and the assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build on 120 regions of 28 requests: the `--app` request of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs` in Speed and Quality at f/8 and f/11 (star centre, label area, step wedge and square patches, line grid, corners, edges and the 24 × 24 region of #440), 16-bit output, ISO 3200, a 1.4 µm sensor, coarse stratified views at K = 4 and 16, mirror borders, four-spot anti-aliasing and AHD without diffraction at ISO 100 and 3200, the dead-leaves and Siemens-star presets, and radiance-, reflectance- and material-function scenes; expected values and noise too on 7 of them, and RAW codes and images of 6 captures. A new test (`test/display-view-speed-material.test.ts`) checks the reported material on ring misses, ring hits and window-cache hits against the query points, that one-material sites of a channel hold one value, and that radiance scenes are not tracked, and locks five Speed renders (caller material scene with mirror borders, a corner part at 16 bits, the stratified estimator, AHD at ISO 3200 and a radiance scene) to hashes recorded before the change. The existing hash locks pass unchanged.
  - **Measured** (Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon, load average 0.8–1.6, `DIST_B=… node scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs --app`, 30 of 294 parts, four repeats per build, builds alternated, against #445): Speed whole-view CPU, extrapolated from the sampled parts, 31.2–32.1 → 29.1–32.0 s at f/8 and 31.0–31.8 → 28.8–30.1 s at f/11 (up to −9 %); part medians 82–87 → 73–84 ms and 83–89 → 76–89 ms; the fastest parts (one material) 7.9–8.5 → 6.7–6.9 µs per display pixel. **Slower:** the Siemens-star centre part, whose samples read two materials and are never replayed, still pays the tracking: alone in a process (`PARTS=1`, six repeats, including JIT warm-up) 230–342 → 246–341 ms at f/8 and 202–262 → 234–271 ms at f/11; without the guard that takes such a sample's statistics inline it was 260–335 ms at f/8. Quality already tracked the material and is unchanged (part medians 133–147 → 131–135 ms at f/8, 136–142 → 135–141 ms at f/11). Browser wall time is not measured here.

- **Display view prepare: the channel response runs the sensor chain once per channel, and a chart, illuminant or illuminance change keeps it (#443).**
  - **What changed:** the channel response (electrons per unit spectral radiance at each spectral node, used by `prepareDisplayView`, `simulateIdealReference` and the capture's tabulated RAW path) ran the facade's environment chain once per spectral node and channel plus once per channel to find the nodes: 63 runs on a 20 nm request. It now runs it once per channel, with unit radiance at every node, and reads each node's electrons from that node's own rate term times the exposure time. The chain is planned as engine-owned, photo-only data, as the capture path already does. The cached response is keyed without the scene bindings (scene, illumination and material profiles), which are checked but never enter its numbers, so a chart, illuminant or illuminance change reuses it; an aperture, shutter, focus or sensor change still recomputes it. ISO and white-balance changes already reused it.
  - **Same bytes:** the per-node sums are compensated sums of per-node terms, so the earlier one-node runs returned exactly that node's term. Samples, RAW codes, diagnostics (including `sceneLookups`) and assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build on 84 cases: the `--app` request of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs` in Speed and Quality at f/8 and f/11 in five regions each, a sequence of prepares with aperture, shutter, chart, illuminant, illuminance, ISO and white-balance changes in Speed and Quality, captured regions with diffraction, a 1.4 µm capture, a DNG, two ideal references and a radiance-function scene. A new test (`test/channel-response.test.ts`) compares each node's value with the earlier one-node runs (`Object.is`) over tiers, pitches, filters, box sizes, apertures, shutters, spectral steps, illuminants, scene kinds and exposure modes, and checks that a chart, illuminant, illuminance or scene change keeps the entry with the maps and bytes of a cold run. The existing hash locks pass unchanged.
  - **Measured** (Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon 2.80 GHz, load average 1.3; the app-request shape of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs --app`, five rounds in one process alternating the two builds, each round a new f-number and shutter): `prepareDisplayView` after an aperture change 378–430 ms → 54–67 ms in Quality and 110–142 ms → 15–19 ms in Speed; after a shutter change 379–420 → 55–69 ms and 112–118 → 18–20 ms; after a chart change 379–435 → 34–39 ms and 108–115 → 6–7 ms; after an illuminant change 370–385 → 35–47 ms and 108–120 → 6–7 ms; after an illuminance change 373–405 → 28–37 ms and 112–124 → 7–11 ms. Each worker of an app prepares once per settings change, so this is time before its first part. Browser wall time is not measured here.
- **AHD demosaic: an interior fast path and no per-pixel allocations (#448).**
  - **What changed:** `createAhdDemosaicTask` read every neighbour through the reflect-101 border mapping and created two closures and four small arrays per pixel. Sites whose neighbourhood lies inside the plane (two sites from the edge for the directional greens, one for the colour differences, homogeneity maps and 3 × 3 sums) now read their neighbours by direct index offsets, where the mapping returns its argument anyway; border sites keep the mapping. The homogeneity loop keeps its 16 differences in locals instead of nested arrays, and the colour matrix entries are read once. Every floating-point expression keeps its operands, order and grouping.
  - **Same bytes:** a new test (`test/processing-ahd-exactness.test.ts`) compares `data` and `direction` bit for bit with a reference copy of the previous algorithm on seeded mosaics of every size from 3 × 3 to 7 × 7 and larger odd sizes, all four CFA phases, and windows of one frame at even and odd offsets including every border, and locks SHA-256 hashes recorded before the change. RAW codes, samples and PNG bytes are identical (SHA-256) for Quality captures (diffraction, four-spot anti-aliasing, AHD) of a 1024² region of a 24 MP frame, the four frame corners with margin and mirror borders, odd offsets, 16-bit output, ISO 3200, a 1.4 µm sensor and whole small frames, and for display views with AHD, which do not run this task.
  - **Measured** in Node, one thread, before and after interleaved: the demosaic stage (`diagnostics.timingsMs.demosaic`) of a 1024² Quality region of a 24 MP frame 594–678 ms → 350–361 ms (−40 %, median 601 → 358 ms; five rounds, fresh processes, the two builds alternated, the second capture of each process). Run directly on the demosaic task (nine rounds, one process): a 1028² plane 450–505 → 272–331 ms (median −38 %), a 100 × 68 plane, the 96 × 64 detail tile with its margin, 3.0 → 1.7 ms (median −43 %). Inside a whole 96 × 64 tile capture the demosaic stage's own timing (11–21 ms) varied more than that and showed no measurable change. Display views, the preview and the Speed preset (bilinear demosaic) do not run this task.
- **Captures with diffraction keep the diffraction profile across calls, and the tabulated RAW loop does less work per site (#449, first part).**
  - **What changed:** `simulateCapture` built the diffraction profile (`createDiffractionLimitedPsfProfile`) on every call, so every region part and detail tile of one request built the same one again. The profiles are now kept across calls by the content of their input (aperture, focal length, f-number, focus, the spectral nodes, pitch, kernel radius, field extent and profile ID), the last 8, as the channel responses already are. Each call gets its own copy of the kept record, as mutable as before, so a caller's change to `request.diffraction.profile` never reaches another call. Metering reads the CIE 1931 ȳ values of its 81 nodes from a table built once instead of evaluating the colour-matching functions for each of the 256 zones. In the tabulated RAW loop, a schedule whose opening and closing boundaries are both simultaneous (the global shutter) reads the exposure window once for the region instead of once per site; rolling-shutter schedules still read it per site. The sensor stage of each verified CFA phase reads its readout constants once, keeps the dark Poisson draw's threshold e^(−mean) while the dark mean repeats, does the ADC arithmetic inline when there is no integral non-linearity (with one it calls `adcTransfer` as before) and reuses one output record; it is checked against the full site computation at the verification sites, as before.
  - **Same bytes:** RAW codes, saturation flags, black levels, saturation codes, samples, PNG and DNG bytes, diagnostics without timings and assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build, on the first and a repeated call in one process, for the app's Quality and Speed requests: the 96 × 64 detail tile, the 192 × 128 detail box, a 1024² region of a 24 MP frame, the frame corners with margin and mirror borders and odd offsets, 16-bit, PNG and DNG output, ISO 3200, a 1.4 µm sensor, whole small frames, a rolling shutter, a radiance-function scene, two captures awaited together, and three display-view parts. The existing hash locks pass unchanged. New tests: `test/capture-set-up-caches.test.ts` (the profile is built once for the parts of a request, any changed input misses and matches a cold run, the cache keeps 8, a caller's change to the returned record does not reach the next call, concurrent captures match sequential ones, the metered luminance keeps its bits, and the exposure window is read once for a global shutter and per site for a rolling one, with the same codes for a still scene) and `test/native-site-readout-flat.test.ts` (every site equals `calculateNativeRawSite` with fixed-pattern noise, digital gain, integral non-linearity, extra read-noise components, saturation and dark means of 0, 8 and 80 e⁻).
  - **Measured** (Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon, load average about 1.3, five rounds alternating the builds, a fresh process per scenario, warm calls): the app's Quality 96 × 64 detail tile median 128 → 115 ms (range 94–170 → 95–145 ms; `timingsMs.raw` median 96 → 92 ms); the 192 × 128 box and a 1024² Quality region within ±3 % (medians 177 → 172 ms and 2520 → 2468 ms). In a CPU profile the profile cache saves 1.5–2 ms and the colour-matching table 3.5–4.5 ms per call. With #446's kernel cache the larger saving the ticket measured on the PSF set-up is already in main.
- **Captures with diffraction keep the expected signal's chart tables across calls (#449, second part).**
  - **What changed:** the tabulated RAW path built the expected signal's tables (the materials, the lattice groups of the query offsets, the per-group, per-channel combined diffraction kernels over every spectral node, and each material's whole-kernel sum) for every `simulateCapture` call, because they were kept per input object and every call builds a new one; every region part and detail tile of one request built the same tables again. For chart materials and caller material scenes the tables are now also kept by their content: every number they are built from (the spectral nodes, the channel responses, the query offsets and weights, the kernel radius and taps, each material's reflectance or radiance and the white radiance), compared bit for bit, the last 4. Each call still gets its own window state (each lattice group's window cache and its position and size, the bands, the site rings and the lookup bookkeeping), and the lattice material rings, which depend on the scene's lookup function, stay per input, so two captures or a capture and a display view awaited together share only the tables, which are never written after they are built. Radiance scenes are not kept by content. Single `simulateDisplayView` calls of one request share the tables the same way; a prepared view already kept them for its renders.
  - **Same bytes:** RAW codes, saturation flags, black levels, saturation codes, samples, PNG and DNG bytes, diagnostics without timings (including `providerEvaluationCount` and `sceneLookups`) and assumptions are identical (SHA-256) to the previous build, on the first and a repeated call in one process, for the app's Quality and Speed requests with a printed label: the 96 × 64 detail tile, the 192 × 128 detail box, a 1024² region of a 24 MP frame, the frame corners with margin and mirror borders and odd offsets, 16-bit, PNG and DNG output, ISO 3200, a 1.4 µm sensor, whole small frames, a rolling shutter, a radiance-function scene, two captures and a display-view part awaited together, and display-view parts of the `--app` request of `scripts/benchmark-display-view.mjs` in Speed and Quality at f/8 and f/11. New tests (`test/expected-signal-table-cache.test.ts`): region parts of one request build the tables once and match a cold run; a changed f-number, pixel pitch, spectral step or material list misses and matches a cold run bit for bit, as does a change of any one number of the tables' input (including the sign of a zero tap); equal inputs read with their windows interleaved give the values and lookup counts of fresh tables; two captures and a display view awaited together give the bytes of sequential runs; the cache keeps 4, least recently used out first.
  - **Measured** (Node 24.21.0, one thread, 4-vCPU Xeon, load average 0.5–1.3, five rounds alternating the builds, a fresh process per build and request, six warm calls after a cold one, against #444): the app's Quality 96 × 64 detail tile 92–174 → 74–135 ms per call, median 116 → 95 ms (`timingsMs.raw` median 94 → 77 ms); the 192 × 128 detail box 136–195 → 125–181 ms, median 160 → 153 ms (`timingsMs.raw` median 124 → 114 ms). The first call per settings still builds the tables (cold calls 301–326 → 306–317 ms and 370–377 → 362–373 ms). The display view's `--app` rows (prepared views, five repeats) are unchanged: part medians 68–73 ms in Speed and 124–135 ms in Quality with both builds. Browser wall time is not measured here.
- **Captures of chart and material scenes: the tabulated RAW path's four verification sites skip re-validating engine-built records, and the diffraction convolution fills its lattice from one material lookup per point (#453).**
  - **What changed:** every tabulated `simulateCapture` call runs one site of each CFA phase through the environment chain to verify the tabulated electrons and to reproduce the readout.
